## [ToR-OT: Autoregressive Transformer Learns Optimal Transport Plans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22218) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce ToR-OT, a transformer-based model that reframes discrete optimal transport (OT) as a sequence generation task and predicts sparse transport plans via autoregressive token regression. Pretrained on a large synthetic corpus, a single model generalizes zero-shot to unseen distribution pairs and heterogeneous cost metrics without retraining. This work explores whether autoregressive transformers can internalize the structure of constrained optimization problems like OT, potentially enabling fast amortized inference that avoids solving an optimization problem at test time. If successful, such learned solvers could accelerate applications in machine learning and beyond where OT is used, though the authors note it is not yet a replacement for classical solvers. ToR-OT decodes only the nonzero entries of the transport plan, exploiting the inherent sparsity of exact discrete OT solutions to avoid dense coupling outputs. On low-dimensional discrete problems, it produces accurate approximate plans and compares favorably with per-instance neural OT baselines, but it is positioned as a step toward understanding learned constrained optimization rather than a production-ready solver.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Optimal transport is a mathematical framework for finding the minimum-cost way to move mass between probability distributions, and classical solvers like Sinkhorn-Knopp remain the gold standard for discrete problems. Amortized optimization uses machine learning to predict solutions to optimization problems by exploiting shared structure across similar instances, enabling rapid inference. Autoregressive transformers generate sequences one token at a time using causal self-attention, and ToR-OT adapts this paradigm to output sparse OT plans as token sequences.

## [Solver-Free Online Scheduling Cuts AI Data Center Energy Costs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22239) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper proposes a solver-free online optimization rule inspired by dual decomposition that assigns each arriving AI data center task a start period to minimize energy cost while respecting operational constraints. Simulations on an A100 GPU trace with 3,456 GPUs and 1,343 tasks show it keeps GPU and CPU usage within capacity and achieves lower cost and much less computation time than a multi-timescale optimization benchmark. AI data centers face a timing mismatch between continuous task arrivals and solver-based optimization that takes time to run, while electricity is priced per settlement period. This approach enables real-time, low-cost scheduling that shifts flexible tasks to low-price periods without delaying high-priority work, potentially reducing operational costs and improving energy efficiency for large-scale GPU clusters. The rule is inspired by dual decomposition, a technique that breaks a difficult optimization problem into smaller subproblems, but here it is used to derive a solver-free online rule that assigns start periods upon task arrival. The evaluation uses a realistic A100 GPU trace, and the method outperforms a multi-timescale benchmark in both cost and computation time, though the paper likely assumes known price signals and task flexibility characteristics.

**Background**: AI data centers run continuous workloads on GPU clusters, and electricity costs vary by settlement period. Traditional optimization-based scheduling uses solvers that can take too long for online decisions, creating a gap between task arrival and scheduling. OfficePrism introduces a benchmark of 420 single-domain enterprise tasks across seven business functions, each with L1–L3 task-structure levels, to evaluate AI agents' ability to handle localized operations. Across 124 model–harness–package configurations, the per-case joint natural-language-and-database pass rate ranges from 22.6% to 76.4%, with a median of 67.9%. ## [Popcornn: Neural Network Path Optimization for Transition State Search on MLIPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22272) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Popcornn, a framework that finds transition states by optimizing continuous neural network paths atop a machine-learned interatomic potential (MLIP) using a parallelized adaptive numerical integrator. It consistently recovers more correct transition states than nudged elastic band and growing string methods across thousands of organic reactions. This work eliminates path discretization artifacts and reduces hyperparameter sensitivity, making transition state search more robust and automation-ready for high-throughput computational chemistry. It could significantly accelerate reaction mechanism studies and catalyst design by leveraging the speed of MLIPs. Popcornn uses second-order potential-energy-surface information via automatic differentiation to enable non-local optimization that overcomes local features, and its infrastructure is flexible in path objective, integration strategy, and optimization algorithm. The method was benchmarked on thousands of organic reactions across multiple MLIPs.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Transition states are high-energy configurations that govern chemical reaction rates, and locating them is a central challenge in computational chemistry. Double-ended search methods like nudged elastic band (NEB) and growing string use discretized paths between reactant and product, but these were designed for density functional theory where energy evaluations are expensive. Machine-learned interatomic potentials (MLIPs) dramatically reduce evaluation cost and provide efficient higher-order derivatives via automatic differentiation, enabling new approaches.

## [Survey: Coding Agents Face the Physical World as Their Test Suite](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22291) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.22291) surveys 'agentic intelligence for the physical world,' examining how coding agents originally built for software repair now write, test, and learn from programs for robots, laboratories, and industrial controllers. It organizes the discussion around three questions: how agents write programs for physical systems, how those programs are tested when no test suite comes with the code, and how agents learn from the results. As coding agents take on more physical tasks, how their programs are judged matters as much as how they are written, and the paper argues progress will depend on a physical counterpart of continuous integration—verification cheap enough to run often and reliable enough to trust. This has implications for researchers in AI, robotics, and software engineering who are pushing agents from codebases into embodied settings. The paper describes each program by where it sits relative to the actuator and by what judges it, covering programs that act as robot policies as well as programs that support policy learning by producing rewards, tasks, and training worlds. For testing, it examines what physical trials cost and what traces, learned judges, simulators, and formal checks can establish in their place, while noting that such evidence is costly to obtain and can itself be wrong.

**Background**: Coding agents are AI systems that combine LLM reasoning with access to coding tools and execution environments, planning changes, editing files, running tests, and iterating on failures—a workflow known as agentic coding. In traditional software engineering, a test suite provides automated, repeatable verification; in the physical world, no such suite exists, and success must be established by running programs on robots, in physics simulators, or through the policies they help train. The paper connects this to embodied intelligence, where intelligent behavior is studied through physical interaction, and to program synthesis, the automated generation of programs from high-level specifications.

The paper introduces executable perceptual memory (EPM), a world-model framework that maintains explicit perceptual state alongside neural latent dynamics during imagination, achieving a 30.37 return on Memory Maze 13x13 at 1M interactions compared to 23.37 for a baseline that uses the same evolving read-out only for policy and value. This work shows that explicitly maintaining and using perceptual memories can significantly improve reward prediction and policy learning in partially observable environments, which is crucial for physical AI agents that must act on information no longer visible or touchable. EPM aligns reward inputs with the transitions that produced them and supervises the reward head on open-loop rollouts of recorded actions; the method also improves control on Memory Maze 9x9 and MiniWorld, with probes showing stronger reward anticipation for remembered out-of-view arrivals.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: World models are learned simulators that let agents predict future states and rewards in a compressed latent space, enabling planning and policy learning without direct environment interaction. Memory Maze is a benchmark for long-term memory in reinforcement learning, where agents must remember cues seen earlier to navigate. EPM adds an explicit, evolving perceptual memory that is read out during imagination to inform reward and value predictions.

## [Agent Trajectories Proposed as Evidence for Software QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22323) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv position paper (2611.22323) proposes analyzing autonomous software agents' execution trajectories as evidence for targeted quality assurance, aiming to surface implicit assumptions and prioritize consequential decisions for human review. The paper argues that final code and passing tests alone do not adequately expose the assumptions agents make during planning, implementation, and testing. As autonomous agents increasingly plan, implement, and test software with limited human oversight, their implicit assumptions can introduce subtle defects that traditional QA misses. This work could shape how organizations review and govern AI-generated software, affecting developers, QA teams, and AI safety practices. The paper is a position/vision piece motivated by industrial experience, and it explicitly acknowledges that trajectories alone do not establish that decisions are justified, outcomes acceptable, or corrective changes effective. It calls for perspectives from requirements engineering, verification and validation, agent systems, causal inference, empirical software engineering, and human factors to develop and evaluate the idea.

## [Visual Backdoors Propagate Through On-Policy Distillation in VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22446) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.22446) challenges the assumption that On-Policy Distillation (OPD) resists backdoor propagation, showing that visual backdoors can still spread from teacher to student models across multiple model families and datasets. The authors identify a critical poisoning threshold above which attack success rate rapidly approaches 100%, and propose a trigger design that enables backdoor propagation with zero explicit trigger injection. This work reveals a subtle security risk in a post-training paradigm increasingly used for vision-language models, suggesting that OPD's apparent resistance to backdoors is illusory and that safety evaluations of distilled VLMs must account for threshold-driven propagation. It affects AI safety researchers, model developers, and anyone deploying distilled vision-language systems. Unlike traditional offline distillation where backdoor propagation is gradual, OPD shows almost no backdoor emergence at low poisoning ratios but a sharp jump to near-100% attack success once a critical threshold is crossed. Propagation is driven not by explicit malicious responses but by the continuous accumulation of anomalous probability mass assigned by the teacher, and the proposed trigger design achieves propagation without any explicit trigger injection.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Knowledge distillation transfers capabilities from a large teacher model to a smaller student model, and prior work has shown that backdoors—hidden trigger-activated behaviors—can pass from teacher to student during conventional offline distillation. On-Policy Distillation (OPD) is a newer post-training method for vision-language models (VLMs) in which the student generates its own response trajectories online and is supervised by the teacher's probability distributions rather than by teacher-generated responses. Because the student's initial outputs are benign and no malicious target responses are provided, OPD was intuitively thought to offer some inherent resistance to backdoor propagation.

MAVE introduces a video-to-blockout framework that combines metric observation, agentic modeling, and evidence-based verification to automatically reconstruct editable Blender scenes from film shots. Alongside it, the authors release MAVEBench, a benchmark of 50 film shots and 416 keyframes, and report improvements of 27.5–41.1 points over vanilla baselines. Manually building 3D blockouts for previsualization is costly and limits both creator workflows and the production of paired training data. By automating this process and releasing a benchmark and dataset, MAVE could make previs more accessible and provide structural conditions for video generation. With DeepSeek-1017, MAVE achieves a score of 73.2 on MAVEBench at an average API cost of only USD 0.054 per shot while reducing total reconstruction time by 53% compared to the strongest vanilla baseline. The authors also construct MAVEData-3K, a dataset of 3,000 blockout-video pairs, which they plan to release publicly.

## [Planner-Hardware Co-Design Exploits Differential Bit-Level Sparsity for Efficient World Model Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22483) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper proposes a planner-hardware co-design that leverages differential bit-level sparsity in quantized JEPA predictors and introduces reference forest sampling to accelerate sampling-based model predictive control (MPC) for embodied agents. The co-design achieves 1.28-1.80x speedup and 38-52% energy reduction over a dense accelerator, and 1.22-1.58x speedup with 16-36% energy savings over prior bit-sparse designs at the same area. Sampling-based MPC with JEPA world models is currently too slow for real-time embodied control, often taking tens of seconds per planning step on high-end GPUs. This work addresses that bottleneck through a tight algorithm-hardware co-design, potentially enabling faster and more energy-efficient planning for robots and other embodied AI systems. The key insight is that the predictor maps close actions to close latents, creating differential bit-level sparsity that hardware can exploit; reference forest sampling couples sampled action sequences to a shared root to manufacture more sparsity while maintaining competitive planning quality. The accelerator is bit-exact and tightly coupled with the planner, which exposes structure for hardware scheduling, and evaluations are on three JEPA world models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: JEPA (Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture) is a framework for learning latent world models that predict in representation space rather than raw pixels. Model predictive control (MPC) is a planning method that optimizes actions by simulating many candidate sequences; sampling-based MPC repeatedly runs the predictor, which is computationally expensive. Bit-level sparsity accelerators skip computations involving zero bits in operands, and prior work has shown benefits for neural network inference. This paper combines these ideas by designing the planner and accelerator together to exploit sparsity that the planner itself creates.

## [Motion-Aware Reward Model Fixes Motion Collapse in Video Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22495) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.22495) identifies a failure mode called 'motion collapse' in reward models for instruction-guided video editing, where composite rewards prefer motion-suppressed candidates in 97% of VE-Bench cases. The authors propose a motion-aware reward model that adds an RGB frame-difference stream and preference training against motion-suppressed and fabricated-motion negatives, reducing the selection rate of motion-suppressed candidates to 0% while achieving a Spearman correlation of 0.57 with human quality ratings. This work exposes a critical flaw in reward models used for video editing and reward-guided fine-tuning, where optimizing for temporal consistency can inadvertently destroy motion. The proposed fix could lead to more reliable evaluation and training of video editing models, directly impacting researchers and practitioners working on AI video generation and editing pipelines. The model combines an RGB frame-difference stream with preference training and a distillation anchor to preserve alignment with human judgments; it detects unseen suppression variants and preserves motion during reward-guided fine-tuning on two editing pipelines. A linear probe of the base reward backbone performs near chance on mild suppression, motivating the explicit inter-frame dynamics input.

The paper introduces MentalGrid, a benchmark for dynamic spatial simulation in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), which reveals significant deficiencies in existing models' ability to simulate evolving spatial states without intermediate visual feedback. To address these gaps, the authors propose a three-stage curriculum learning framework using 16k specialized samples, yielding SpatialWalker, which achieves a 33.8% average improvement over its base model and surpasses proprietary models like Gemini-3-Flash in spatial simulation. This work addresses an underexplored area of dynamic spatial simulation, which is crucial for broader spatial intelligence in AI systems. The proposed benchmark and curriculum learning approach could significantly advance multimodal reasoning research and lead to more capable models for tasks requiring mental simulation of spatial dynamics. The benchmark evaluation shows that performance is constrained by perceptual limitations in simple simulation scenarios and hindered by inadequate multi-step reasoning as complexity increases. SpatialWalker also demonstrates robust generalization, maintaining a 4.9% average gain on out-of-domain benchmarks, highlighting the pivotal role of spatial simulation as a fundamental building block for broader spatial intelligence.

## [JointGV: Graph-Visual Correction Reduces Drift in Robot World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22543) ⭐️ 7.0/10

JointGV, a new plug-in method, couples task-conditioned scene graph dynamics with visual world model predictions to correct recursive drift in long-horizon robot planning. It reduces joint-position error by 11–28% over 32-step rollouts and cuts real-robot pole-angle error from 12.56° to 6.69° when attached to DINO-WM. Visual world models are increasingly used for robot planning, but recursive errors limit their reliability over long horizons. JointGV offers a general, model-agnostic correction that improves both prediction accuracy and control success without retraining the underlying visual model, potentially making world-model-based planning more practical for real-world robotics. JointGV is based on a cross-granularity dynamics hypothesis: graph-node and visual-state changes describe the same physical transition despite different representations. It computes a bounded residual from graph-motion context and graph-predicted node positions, and gradients from graph learning and correction do not propagate into the original visual dynamics. It was evaluated on DINO-WM and DreamerV3 across DMC Cheetah Run, Walker Walk, and Finger Turn Hard, and on real-robot inverted-pendulum sequences.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Visual world models predict future visual states conditioned on actions, enabling robot planning and control. However, small errors in predicted entity poses and spatial relations accumulate recursively over long horizons, causing drift. Scene graphs represent objects and their relations as nodes and edges, and graph neural networks can model their dynamics. JointGV combines these two representations to correct drift without altering the visual model.

## [Evolving Agent Safety Harnesses with Adaptive Injection Stressors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22583) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces "Evolution Under Fire," a staged framework that co-evolves executable safety harnesses and adaptive stressors to defend tool-using language-model agents against prompt injection. On AgentDyn with GPT-4o-mini, the evolved harness reduces attack success from 50.4% to 3.0% while improving clean and attacked utility. Prompt injection is a critical security threat for LLM agents that act on untrusted emails, webpages, and documents, and existing defenses are largely hand-designed and static. This work shows that automated, evolution-based harness design can achieve a stronger security-utility trade-off than reproduced defenses, potentially reducing the need for repeated manual redesign. The framework uses utility-constrained, Pareto-scheduled tree search to evolve complete executable safety harnesses from execution evidence while keeping the task model, benchmark, tools, and evaluator fixed; adaptive stressor evolution then discovers utility-preserving attacks to guide further harness evolution. On Agent-SafetyBench with Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, attack success dropped from 82.6% to 62.0%, and evolved harnesses transferred without further evolution to Qwen3.5-35B-A3B, reducing attack success from 32.1% to 0.9% while improving attacked-task utility.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Tool-using LLM agents can receive malicious instructions and legitimate task directives through the same interaction channel, such as emails or webpages, making prompt injection a central security challenge. AgentDyn is a dynamic benchmark for evaluating LLM agent security via indirect prompt injection, featuring 60 open-ended user tasks and 560 injection test cases across Shopping, GitHub, and Daily Life scenarios. Pareto-scheduled tree search is an evolutionary algorithm that balances multiple objectives, here security and utility, by exploring a tree of candidate solutions guided by Pareto optimality.

## [Narrative Coherence Boosts LLM Honeypot Deception Against LLM Attackers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22589) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new paper proposes a narrative deception strategy for LLM-driven honeypots, where the defender maps each attacker command to a MITRE ATT&CK technique, selects a compatible scenario from a MITRE Engage-based catalogue, and deploys mutually referential artefacts (files, processes, services, logs) on an emulated Ubuntu host during an ongoing intrusion. In a campaign of 1,150 SSH sessions against an autonomous LLM attacker, coherent scenarios raised the median session length of advanced attackers from 40 to 165 commands and reduced abandonment from 86% to 52%. As autonomous LLM-driven offensive agents become realistic adversaries, this work shows that deception quality—not just emulator fidelity—can meaningfully extend attacker engagement, which is critical for threat intelligence gathering and defender learning. The findings suggest honeypot design should focus on sustaining meaningful findings rather than merely mimicking system behavior. The study compared four conditions of increasing sophistication (Static, Same-TTP, Multi-TTP, and Chain) across four open-weight attacker models, four defender models, and three attacker skill levels, finding that engagement increased significantly at every step with comparable inference cost. Nearly all sessions ended through loss of interest rather than detection, indicating that sustaining meaningful findings is more important than perfect emulation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Honeypots are decoy systems designed to attract and observe attackers, and LLMs now enable them to generate dynamic responses and artefacts on demand. MITRE ATT&CK is a knowledge base of adversary tactics and techniques, while MITRE Engage is a framework for planning adversary engagement and deception operations. This paper evaluates whether artefacts that jointly describe a coherent event retain an attacker better than unrelated ones, using a real SSH session between an LLM-powered defender and an autonomous LLM attacker.

FVScore is a neuro-symbolic framework that compiles generated videos into explicit and implicit propositions, grounds them with specialized perceptual tools, and evaluates confidence traces using soft temporal operators inspired by Linear Temporal Logic. <a id="item-167"></a>
## [Study quantifies detail loss in vision-language models on large images, shows tiling and zooming recover accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22709) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.22709) systematically measures how much small detail is lost when online vision-language models process large images, testing seven models from four vendors. It finds that the smallest text a model can read half the time grows from 6 pixels or less on small images to a predicted 10–13 pixels on a 7680×4320 photo at default settings, and that splitting images into up to nine tiles raises accuracy by 11.1–14.5 percentage points for Claude and Gemini, while lifting GPT-6.1 Sol from 50.7% to 83.7% by avoiding rejections. This work exposes a practical, under-examined failure mode in multimodal AI deployment: services often downscale or reject large images before the model sees them, silently erasing critical details. The findings offer actionable guidance—such as tiling or two-step zooming—for practitioners building agents that rely on high-resolution imagery, and highlight differing failure behaviors between vendors that could affect trust and reliability. A two-step zoom approach, where the model first names the region it needs, matches or beats tiling on Gemini and comes within 2.5 points on Claude while using about 40% of the image tokens. When details are lost, Gemini gives a confident wrong answer in 43–48% of cases versus only 5–9% for Claude, and tiling only after an unsure reply comes within 1.4 points of always tiling with 62–70% of the tokens.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) process images by converting them into tokens, and online services often resize or reject images that exceed size limits to control cost and latency. This downscaling can make small objects—like a car 20 pixels wide in an 8000×6000 drone photo—nearly invisible to the model. Tiling splits a large image into smaller patches that are processed separately, while zooming lets the model request a closer look at a specific region, both aiming to preserve detail without exceeding input limits.

## [Unauthenticated Config Attack Hijacks LLM Social Robot Personality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22761) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers demonstrated an unauthenticated network-exposed configuration attack on the Pollen Robotics Reachy Mini Lite robot, allowing an attacker to replace personality settings and the cloud LLM's system prompt via a management interface without any adversarial conversational input. While eleven prompt-injection attempts across five categories were resisted, both configuration-layer attack paths produced observable behavioral changes on physical hardware. This work reveals a critical deployment gap in embodied AI security: inference-layer refusal mechanisms cannot prevent unauthorized changes to model instructions at the configuration layer, exposing robot owners to surveillance via onboard cameras and microphones as well as social engineering of nearby users. It highlights that LLM-controlled social robots need end-to-end security, not just prompt-injection defenses. The weaknesses map to CWE-306 (Missing Authentication for a Critical Function) and CWE-1327 (Binding to an Unrestricted IP Address), with a reported CVSS v3.1 score of 6.5 (Medium); a configuration endpoint also accepts cross-origin requests without CORS preflight (CWE-352, 6.5 Medium), and the persistent-memory sink stores content without enforcing its stated restrictions. A source review of 59 public repositories found missing authentication in 10 of 11 source-verifiable genuine applications (11 of 11 including a framework-default case), indicating recurrence within the sample rather than prevalence in commercial products.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Reachy Mini Lite is a compact, open-source desktop humanoid robot from Pollen Robotics (part of Hugging Face), designed for learning and experimenting with robotics and AI; it uses a cloud LLM to drive conversational behavior. CWE-306 describes missing authentication for critical functionality, while CWE-1327 describes binding a service to an unrestricted IP address such as 0.0.0.0, which can expose it to network attackers. Prompt injection is an attack where adversarial input manipulates an LLM's instructions, but this research shows that attacking the configuration layer directly can bypass such conversational defenses.

## [StitchRAG: Diffusion-based stitching for compact RAG contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22766) ⭐️ 7.0/10

StitchRAG introduces a framework that dynamically compresses retrieved passages and stitches key sentences together using text diffusion to form compact, evidence-preserving contexts for retrieval-augmented generation. Experiments on five open-domain QA benchmarks show it consistently outperforms multi-step and compression baselines. Long retrieved contexts in RAG incur computational overhead and can degrade performance due to noisy or irrelevant content, so a method that produces compact yet faithful contexts could improve both efficiency and answer accuracy for LLM-based QA systems. This work also demonstrates a novel application of diffusion models to context compression, potentially inspiring further cross-pollination between generative modeling and retrieval pipelines. StitchRAG adapts the size-reduction ratio per passage by formulating a utility maximization that trades off retained information against computation cost, then extracts answer candidates and query-related keywords as anchors and collects sentences containing them under the derived size constraint. Finally, text diffusion is used to stitch the collected sentences into a coherent context; ablations confirm the effectiveness of dynamic size allocation, anchor preservation, and diffusion-based stitching.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) enhances large language models by retrieving external documents to ground their outputs, but the retrieved contexts can be long and noisy. Context compression aims to shorten these contexts while preserving key evidence, and prior methods often use fixed compression ratios or extractive selection. Diffusion models, originally popular for image generation, are increasingly being adapted for text generation and editing, enabling non-autoregressive refinement of sequences.

## [ForceBody Dataset Pairs Ground Truth Forces with Parametric Body Model and Torque Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22769) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ForceBody introduces the first dataset that brings ground truth force supervision into a parametric body representation, pairing the SKEL body model with ground reaction forces and inverse-dynamics joint torques across 10,386 motion trials (9.7M frames, 26.9 hours) from 140 subjects. Each torque label includes a per-frame, per-joint uncertainty annotation obtained via Monte Carlo sampling through the inverse dynamics pipeline. This dataset fills a critical gap in physics-aware motion learning by making force and torque data compatible with modern parametric body models used in biomechanics, robotics, and animation. The inclusion of per-sample torque uncertainty enables more reliable training and evaluation of models that predict physical quantities from motion. The authors benchmark six architectures (MLP, Conv1D, LSTM, GRU, Mamba2, Transformer) on ground reaction force and joint torque prediction from SKEL motion. As an example use of the released uncertainty, finetuning a Transformer with per-sample uncertainty weighting reduces the mean absolute error of joint torque estimation by 5%.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Parametric body models like SKEL and SMPL provide a low-dimensional representation of human shape and pose, and are widely used in learning-based motion reconstruction and generation. Inverse dynamics computes joint torques from kinematics (positions, velocities, accelerations) and body inertial parameters, but the resulting torques are sensitive to noise in the input kinematics. Existing motion datasets with force annotations often use incompatible body representations and do not quantify torque reliability.

## [NexSight: Verifiable Tasks for Agentic Long-Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22790) ⭐️ 7.0/10

NexSight introduces verifiable tasks and an agentic pipeline called SightForge to evaluate and train models on fine-grained long-video understanding. The pipeline accepts a task only if a verifier blind to the draft answer can reconstruct it from densely reacquired evidence and counterexample checks fail to refute it, yielding SightLine, a benchmark of 310 evidence-traceable tasks from 108 long videos. This work addresses a hard measurement problem in long-video QA by requiring evidence reconstruction and counterexample checks, enabling step-level supervision from verified solver trajectories. It shows that with under 6K examples, a 35B model nearly doubles its base model's interactive accuracy and outperforms a 397B model, suggesting verifiable data can significantly boost agentic video understanding. The strongest model reaches only 54.52% on SightLine but 79.35% when given annotated evidence, highlighting the difficulty of evidence search. Sight-35B, trained with under 6K examples, improves interactive accuracy from 21.29% to 42.26%, begins using explicit spatial inspection after post-training, and gains over 8 points on two external long-video benchmarks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Long-video understanding involves answering fine-grained questions about events scattered across hours of footage, such as counts, order, or exact timing. Agentic models that decide when and where to look promise to find these moments, but their ability is hard to measure and learn because sparse evidence may not establish counts and answer-only supervision does not teach evidence search. Verifiable benchmarks, which use source-grounded rubrics or evidence traces, are emerging as a way to evaluate complex reasoning tasks reliably.

<a id="item-172"></a>
## [WATERFALL: Evolutionary Workflow Automates Reward Discovery in POMDPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22854) ⭐️ 7.0/10

WATERFALL introduces an iterative population-based workflow for automated reward discovery in reinforcement learning, specifically targeting partially observable environments without relying on goal-conditioning or expert demonstrations. It synthesizes diverse programmatic reward candidates via persona-conditioned generation, evaluates them using a Swiss-system evolutionary tournament judged by vision-language models on raw visual rollouts, and iteratively mutates, escalates, or prunes candidates based on longitudinal assessment histories. Reward design remains a central bottleneck in reinforcement learning, especially in sparse-reward, long-horizon, and partially observable settings. WATERFALL's ability to discover reward programs without privileged information could significantly reduce manual reward engineering and enable autonomous learning in complex, realistic environments where task objectives are not explicitly specified. WATERFALL is evaluated across fully observable and reveal-gated partially observable environments ranging from discrete to continuous, outperforming sparse-reward RL, intrinsic-motivation, and foundation-model reward-synthesis baselines, most clearly in the POMDP regime. Ablations show that visual evidence, tournament-based selection, and iterative refinement are jointly important, and the approach enforces a contract that separates autonomous reward discovery from benchmark-privileged reward shaping.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning (RL) trains agents to make decisions by rewarding desired behaviors, but designing effective reward functions is notoriously difficult and often requires manual engineering. Partially observable Markov decision processes (POMDPs) model settings where the agent cannot directly observe the full state of the environment, making reward design even harder because task-relevant information must be inferred from incomplete observations. Recent approaches use foundation models to automate reward design, but most assume access to goal specifications, task descriptions, or expert demonstrations, which are often unavailable in POMDPs. Population-based training and evolutionary algorithms maintain and evolve multiple candidate solutions, offering a way to explore reward programs without gradient-based optimization.

## [AgentWorld: Agent-Centric Latent World Models for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22880) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2611.22880) introduces AgentWorld, an agent-centric vision-language-action (VLA) world model that uses discrete agent tokens for joint reasoning and trajectory planning instead of generating dense pixel-level future frames. Each token is dynamically grounded to a surrounding agent and encodes its semantic identity, 3D geometry, motion, and interaction context, learned through explicit geometric grounding, reasoning-oriented supervised fine-tuning, and agent-oriented reinforcement learning. Most world VLA models for autonomous driving generate future frames at the pixel level, which preserves many driving-irrelevant appearance details and creates a performance bottleneck because only a small portion of tokens encode planning-critical factors. AgentWorld reframes the question of what world state to reason over, potentially improving planning efficiency and interpretability for end-to-end driving systems. The model uses discrete agent tokens that are dynamically grounded to surrounding agents, capturing object state such as semantic identity, 3D geometry, motion, and interaction context. Experiments in open-loop and closed-loop settings under diverse driving scenarios show enhanced planning capabilities and interpretable agent-centric world states, though the work is an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) models integrate visual perception, language understanding, and action generation to enable end-to-end autonomous driving. World models in this context allow systems to reason about imagined futures rather than merely reacting to current observations. Latent world models compress high-dimensional sensory inputs into compact latent states to support downstream prediction and planning, and agent-centric representations focus on object-level interactions rather than dense pixel-level details.

## [LLM agents can exceed their first stop in long-horizon vulnerability exploitation with context compaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22888) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper investigates why LLM agents stop early during long-horizon vulnerability exploitation and proposes SCoRe, a context compaction method that compacts context at stop bursts and marks impossibility judgments as unproven. On ExploitBench with MiMo-V2.6-Pro, SCoRe achieves a 67.0% capability coverage score at 2,000 turns, surpassing the 32.4% under the original stopping policy and the 58.6% computed for GPT-5.5 from 300-turn reference results. This work shows that an agent's first stop does not mark its exploitation limit, suggesting that test-time scaling with better context management can elicit substantial additional capability from LLM agents in security-critical tasks. It has implications for AI security research, agent design, and the evaluation of long-horizon autonomous systems. The study identifies two patterns associated with less productive continuation: an increasing proportion of stop responses within the same context and earlier stopping linked to summaries that retain impossibility judgments about unachieved capabilities. SCoRe improves verified capability over summary-based continuation and other context-management baselines, though gains diminish over longer execution and results are limited to specific models and the ExploitBench benchmark.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Vulnerability exploitation tests whether language model agents can develop working exploits from known software flaws. ExploitBench is a benchmark that measures how far AI agents climb in exploitation, from reaching vulnerable code to achieving arbitrary code execution. Test-time scaling, which allocates more compute during inference, can improve agent performance, but agents often stop prematurely even with budget remaining. Context compaction summarizes earlier parts of a long interaction so the agent can continue working within its context window.

## [Projected Penalty-Gradient Tracking for Byzantine-Resilient Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22929) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a projected penalty-gradient tracking framework that maintains Byzantine resilience even when workers upload compressed updates, and proposes two algorithms: FRPG-CS (fault-resilient proximal gradient with consistent sparsification) and LAG-FRPG (lazily aggregated FRPG). This work addresses a critical gap in Byzantine-resilient federated learning by enabling compression without sacrificing robustness, which is essential for deploying federated learning in communication-constrained and adversarial environments. For smooth and strongly convex losses, the authors establish a finite-time bound on the expected objective gap where the Byzantine error floor is independent of the compressor, and they also bound the expected number of lazy uploads; experiments on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST show the algorithms achieve the highest accuracy among twelve methods under the Min-Max attack.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Federated learning enables multiple workers to collaboratively train a model without sharing raw data, but it is vulnerable to Byzantine workers that can send arbitrary updates to corrupt the training. Penalty-based methods bound the influence of each worker by using a penalty function with bounded gradients, but this bound is lost when uploads are compressed because a Byzantine worker can accumulate an unbounded state at the server. Communication efficiency is often achieved via compression techniques such as sparsification or quantization, which are contractive compressors that reduce upload size but can interact poorly with Byzantine resilience mechanisms.

## [BReaD: Diffusion Guidance Sampling for High-Dimensional Black-Box Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22965) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces BReaD (Black-box Reward-guided Sampling of Diffusion Model), a guidance sampling framework that optimizes black-box rewards without requiring labeled data or exact reward gradients. It uses a Gaussian process as a differentiable surrogate and actively queries labels for generated samples, with theoretical analysis of the total-variation distance between generated and target distributions. This approach addresses key limitations of Bayesian optimization and prior diffusion-based methods in high-dimensional spaces, potentially enabling efficient optimization in scientific discovery and image generation with limited evaluations. It broadens the applicability of diffusion models to real-world black-box scenarios where gradients and labels are scarce. BReaD uses Upper Confidence Bound (UCB) as guidance signals to balance exploration and exploitation during generation, and numerical experiments demonstrate effectiveness in low-data scenarios for scientific discovery and image generation tasks. The theoretical analysis bounds the total-variation distance between the generated and target distributions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Black-box optimization involves optimizing objectives where only limited evaluations are available and gradients are inaccessible. Bayesian optimization is a standard framework but struggles in high dimensions, while recent diffusion-based methods either require large labeled datasets for pre-training or assume access to differentiable reward gradients. BReaD combines diffusion models with Gaussian process surrogates to overcome these requirements.

## [Large Language Models Have Not Yet Transformed Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22975) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.22975) analyzes 2.5 million full-text papers from 2015 to 2025 across five disciplines and finds that most pre-2023 scientific trends continued after the emergence of LLMs, with only limited detectable breaks. Notable exceptions include accelerated growth in computer science coauthor teams in Asia and apparent declines in biomedical topic diversity that largely reflect standardization in article formats. This large-scale empirical study provides a data-driven counterpoint to hype about LLMs transforming science, suggesting that their impact on scientific practices has been more incremental than revolutionary so far. It offers valuable evidence for researchers, funders, and policymakers monitoring the health of global science as LLM adoption deepens. The study traces trends across lexical, structural, epistemological, and social dimensions, but its findings are based on five disciplines and may not generalize to all fields. The authors note that apparent declines in biomedical topic diversity are largely an artifact of standardized article formats, highlighting the need to distinguish genuine shifts from formatting changes.

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT, released in late 2022, have been widely predicted to accelerate and transform scientific research by assisting with writing, analysis, and idea generation. A new arXiv paper (2611.22989) empirically analyzed 36 runs of web research agents built on OpenAI and Anthropic models, finding that when given the same priority (growth or resilience), the agents used different criteria and produced divergent company rankings and recommendations for Microsoft and Alphabet. <a id="item-179"></a>
## [DGSP: Planner-Probing Perception Focuses High-Res Compute on Decision-Critical Regions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23030) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.23030v2) introduces Decision-Guided Speculative Perception (DGSP), which probes the planner with hypothetical objects to build a decision-sensitivity field and then applies high-resolution perception only where an unseen object could change the planner's action. For two planner classes, the authors prove DGSP returns exactly the same action as whole-frame high-resolution perception under stated conditions, and on three driving datasets it matches that action on about 98% of frames using roughly a quarter of the pixels. This work shifts adaptive perception from image-driven cues to planner-driven decision sensitivity, which could make safety-critical autonomous systems more efficient and more reliable by spending computation where it actually affects driving decisions. It offers a theoretical guarantee for two planner classes and empirical gains in repairing decision-relevant misses, suggesting a path toward deployable adaptive-resolution perception in autonomous driving. DGSP treats the low-resolution scene and its action as a draft, probes the planner to obtain a decision-sensitivity field, and verifies only that field and the support (detections the current action rests on) at high resolution, iterating until the action is stable. The theoretical guarantee requires crop detections to match whole-frame detections and the crops to cover the field, conditions real images meet only approximately; at the pixel cost of uniform downscaling, DGSP repairs more draft disagreements, brakes falsely less often, and recovers more decision-relevant objects.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Autonomous driving stacks typically separate perception (detecting objects from sensor data) from planning (deciding the vehicle's action). Adaptive-resolution perception aims to save computation by processing only parts of the image at high resolution, but existing methods choose regions based on image cues, which may miss objects that low resolution failed to detect or may waste effort on objects irrelevant to the planner. DGSP instead asks the planner which locations would matter by inserting hypothetical objects and observing whether the planned action changes.

<a id="item-180"></a>
## [Learning to Undo: Transfer RL via State Space Transformations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23081) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes an algorithm that learns an undo map between a source and target MDP by matching state feature statistics, then uses it to transfer the source policy to the target. It provides theoretical guarantees showing strictly better sample complexity than tabula rasa RL when the undo map is linear and the source is linearly-Q* realizable, and demonstrates improved sample efficiency on continuous control and Atari tasks. This work offers a principled framework for transfer reinforcement learning with provable benefits, which could lead to more sample-efficient agents in new environments by exploiting shared structure between tasks. It is particularly relevant for RL researchers working on theory and sample complexity. The theoretical analysis assumes a linear undo map and linear-Q* realizability of the source MDP, but empirical results suggest the benefits extend beyond this regime. The method relies on matching state feature statistics gathered from both MDPs to learn the map.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning (RL) is often formalized as a Markov decision process (MDP), where an agent learns to maximize cumulative reward through interactions. Transfer learning in RL aims to reuse knowledge from a source task to speed up learning in a related target task. The linear-Q* realizability assumption means the optimal action-value function can be represented as a linear function of given state features, a common setting in theoretical RL analysis.

<a id="item-181"></a>
## [BugGen 2.0 splits RTL bug synthesis between deterministic algorithms and LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23091) ⭐️ 7.0/10

BugGen 2.0 introduces a hybrid approach where deterministic algorithms handle structural analysis, target selection, and validation, while LLMs focus on localized semantic rewriting. Across four OpenTitan IP blocks and FabScalar with three LLMs, it meets every requested quota of compilable bug scenarios causing at least two test failures, representing 14-16 of 16 configured mutation types. This work lowers inference and simulation costs for generating ground-truth RTL bug datasets, which are essential for AI-assisted debugging and testbench grading. By improving consistency across models and designs, it could accelerate dataset creation for hardware verification and EDA research. BugGen 2.0 reduces token use by 6.8x-15.4x relative to BugGen 1.0 and 1.5x-7.9x relative to Firefly in same-model comparisons. It also skips 63.6% of AES and 33.9% of FabScalar variant simulations by estimating acceptance for alternative mutations, with full simulation showing 77.7% and 95.2% acceptance rates and a 1.09% estimation error on FabScalar.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: RTL (register-transfer level) is a design abstraction for synchronous digital circuits, describing data flow between registers and logical operations. OpenTitan is an open-source silicon root-of-trust project providing reusable IP blocks, while FabScalar is a toolset for automatically generating synthesizable RTL for superscalar processor cores. Bug injection into RTL is used to create datasets for debugging and to measure testbench detection capability.

<a id="item-182"></a>
## [Tree-grams: Hierarchical n-gram models rival transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23093) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce tree-grams, autoregressive hierarchical n-gram models that build the conditional probability tensor through compositions of order-2 and order-3 tensors, controlled by context window n and rank d. They outperform n-gram baselines, compete with transformers, and enable robust μP hyperparameter transfer and up to 95% topology pruning. Tree-grams offer an interpretable and systematically scalable family of language approximators, bridging synthetic and realistic language modeling. Their ability to control horizon and capacity explicitly makes them valuable for studying hyperparameter transfer and model pruning, with implications for efficient training of large models. The model's capacity is controlled by a rank hyperparameter d, while n sets the maximal context window. When allowed to route across candidate topologies, tree-grams exhibit emergent sparsity, allowing pruning of up to 95% of topologies without significant performance loss; entropy and effective correlation range are shown to control the performance gap after fine-tuning.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: N-gram language models predict the next word based on a fixed window of previous words, but they have largely been superseded by neural models like transformers. Hierarchical models have been used as synthetic testbeds for studying language, but their relevance to realistic corpora was underexplored. μP (maximal update parametrization) is a technique that stabilizes optimal hyperparameters across model sizes, enabling zero-shot hyperparameter transfer from small to large models.

<a id="item-183"></a>
## [TriAgent: Multi-Agent System Detects and Repairs Documentation Drift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23142) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper presents TriAgent, a deployed multi-agent system that continuously extracts explicit and implicit signals to detect and repair documentation drift. Over 43 days of shadow-mode operation, it triaged 499 tickets, surfaced 92 documentation gaps, and achieved 81.1% correctness on verifiable assertions according to a panel of LLM judges. Documentation drift is increasingly critical as LLM-based agents consume documentation as their primary interface, and stale information can propagate as authoritative. TriAgent's real-world evaluation demonstrates a practical approach to maintaining documentation quality in fast-paced software development, potentially reducing support burden and improving agent reliability. The system uses a triage agent to route signals, and in a blinded evaluation, judges preferred TriAgent's responses over human engineers' in 70.5% of length-comparable pairs. After repairing a gap, the agent's post-repair answers were preferred 71.4% of the time (n=35), and a human audit confirmed alignment on self-improvement.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Documentation drift occurs when documentation no longer matches the software's behavior, often accumulating silently as code evolves. Shadow-mode deployment is a testing practice where a new system runs in parallel with production, processing real data without affecting end users. Multi-agent systems use specialized agents, such as a triage agent, to divide and route tasks for complex workflows.

<a id="item-184"></a>
## [Stratified Policies: Allocating Inference Budget Across LoRA Populations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23154) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces stratified policies, a method that allocates the inference-time sample budget across a population of k learned LoRA adapters on a shared language model backbone, with each adapter contributing one response to a k-sample objective optimized for pass@k. The authors provide theoretical motivation in the linear setting showing that a population of LoRAs can achieve zero best-of-k approximation error even when every single LoRA has strictly positive prediction error, and empirically demonstrate consistent pass@k improvements over a single-policy baseline across multiple reasoning tasks. This work challenges the common practice of repeated i.i.d. sampling from a single policy for inference-time scaling, suggesting that distributing samples across complementary policies can use the sample budget more effectively. If validated broadly, it could influence how practitioners design inference-time scaling systems for LLMs, particularly in reasoning-heavy applications where pass@k is a key metric. The method uses separate LoRA adapters on a shared backbone, and the theoretical analysis is limited to the linear setting, which may not fully capture deep network behavior. Empirical gains are shown on multiple reasoning tasks, but the paper does not specify the model sizes, task details, or the exact magnitude of improvements.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Inference-time scaling improves LLM performance by generating multiple candidate responses and selecting or evaluating them collectively, often measured by pass@k—the probability that at least one of k samples is correct. LoRA is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique that freezes the pre-trained model weights and injects small trainable low-rank matrices into each layer, enabling multiple lightweight adapters to share a single backbone. This paper combines these ideas by training a population of LoRA adapters and using each to produce one sample at inference time.

<a id="item-185"></a>
## [Attribution Through Decoder Differential Improves Explanations for Structured Outputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23161) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.23161) proposes attributing model predictions through the decoder differential rather than raw coordinates, showing that this avoids misleading explanations for structured outputs such as angles, gaze directions, and rotations. The method reduces to a scaled tangent projection for normalization decoders and is validated on synthetic tasks, MPIIGaze, and PASCAL3D+. This work addresses a real flaw in attribution methods for structured outputs, where standard techniques can credit inputs for changes the decoder discards, leading to explanations that highlight irrelevant features. It is valuable for explainable AI practitioners working with computer vision models that predict angles, rotations, or other structured quantities. On synthetic tasks with analytically known relevance, ambient attribution assigns up to 72% of its mass to an irrelevant region; on MPIIGaze, tangent Grad-CAM produces 44% greater prediction displacement under 10% pixel deletion; and on PASCAL3D+ with a 6D rotation decoder, the full differential improves mean deletion displacement by 14.1 degrees over the ambient coordinate sum. The authors note that an invariant attribution can still miss inputs that drive the prediction, so invariance and deletion performance should each be checked.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 19, 05:00

**Background**: Attribution methods like Integrated Gradients explain model predictions by assigning importance scores to input features, but they are typically applied to the raw output coordinates of a network. Many computer vision models predict structured quantities such as angles or rotations indirectly: they output raw coordinates that a fixed decoder (e.g., normalization) maps to the final prediction. Applying attribution to these raw coordinates can credit inputs for changes that the decoder discards, such as rescaling a vector when only its direction matters.
